● Now I have a thorough picture of the project. Let me give you the full review.

  ---
  Project Review: Poet - Arabic Poetry Generation System

  What it is

  A pipeline-based system for generating classical Arabic poetry that attempts
  strict compliance with traditional Arabic prosody rules (ةيرعشلا روحبلا). The
  pipeline flows:

  User prompt
    → ConstraintParser (LLM: extract meter, qafiya, theme, etc.)
    → QafiyaSelector (LLM: select/confirm rhyme letter)
    → BahrSelector (LLM: select/confirm bahr/meter)
    → DataEnricher (corpus RAG + optional web search for examples)
    → SimplePoemGenerator (LLM: generate poem with tashkeel)
    → PoemEvaluator (LLM + bohour lib: check prosody & qafiya)
    → RefinerChain × N iterations (LLM: fix violations)
    → Harmony compiler (LLM: summarize the whole run)

  What's strong

  - Clean node architecture — YAML-config-driven pipeline, easy to
  add/remove/reorder steps
  - Bilingual prompts — full Arabic + English template set in parallel
  - Bohour library integration — proper structural representation of all 16
  classical Arabic meters with tafeelat patterns and allowed zehafs/elals
  - Multi-provider LLM abstraction — Groq, OpenAI, Anthropic, vLLM, Gemini,
  HuggingFace, Mock
  - Corpus RAG — retrieves real classical examples matching the requested
  meter/rhyme for in-context learning
  - BestOfN search — generates N candidates and selects the best one
  - Rich Constraints model — captures meter, qafiya, harakah, qafiya type
  (mutawatir/mutaradif/etc.), tone, theme, imagery, register, era

  Core problems (why it stalled)

  1. Prosody validation is entirely LLM-based and circular
  ProsodyEvaluator._validate_bait_with_llm asks an LLM "is this verse in meter
  X?" — the same type of model that generated it. The bohour library is only
  used to extract the tafeelat description as a string, not for actual
  algorithmic checking. This means the validation signal is unreliable and the
  refinement loop has no ground truth to converge toward.

  2. The generation prompt doesn't teach meter structure
  simple_poem_generation.yaml tells the model to follow the bahr but only passes
   meter (name) and meeter_tafeelat (tafeelat string). It doesn't pass the
  actual syllabic pattern (1/0 notation), zehafs, aruz/dharb constraints. Weaker
   models guessed and got it wrong.

  3. Refinement feedback is vague
  The prosody refiner gets changes_made / meter_compliance / rhyme_compliance
  but no verse-by-verse breakdown of what syllables are wrong. Without precise
  error location, the LLM can't fix it reliably.

  4. target_quality: 1 is unreachable with weak models
  The config sets target_quality: 1.0 (perfect score), meaning the loop always
  runs all 3 iterations without early exit.

  5. Configs default to Groq/Llama, not Claude
  The default and most of the configs use
  meta-llama/llama-4-scout-17b-16e-instruct via Groq. Arabic classical prosody
  is a highly specialized domain where stronger models matter enormously.

  ---
  Revival Strategy

  The architecture is actually solid. With Claude 3.5/Claude 4 the weak links
  become fixable. Here's what I'd tackle:

  High impact, low effort

  1. Switch to claude-sonnet-4-6 as default LLM — the existing Anthropic adapter
   works, just update the config. Claude is dramatically better at Arabic
  prosody.
  2. Add extended_thinking to the refinement nodes — prosody correction is a
  reasoning-heavy task. Budget tokens for thinking in the ProsodyRefiner and
  PoemEvaluator.
  3. Improve the generation prompt — pass the full syllabic pattern
  (all_baits_combinations_patterns[0]) directly into the prompt, not just the
  tafeelat name string. "نْلُعِافَتَمُ نْلُعِفَ" is not as actionable as 0110 
  01110.
  4. Fix prosody evaluation — the bohour library can algorithmically check
  syllabic patterns. bahr_instance.all_baits_combinations_patterns already has
  the valid binary patterns. The arudi_style module converts verses to
  waqf/harakah sequences. We should match algorithmically and only use LLM as a
  fallback.

  Medium effort, high payoff

  5. Richer refinement feedback — on failed baits, compute the actual scanned
  pattern vs. expected pattern using the bohour library and pass the diff to the
   refiner LLM.
  6. Generation with chain-of-thought — restructure the generation prompt to ask
   the model to plan the prosodic structure first (syllable count per shatr,
  valid zehafs to use) before writing the verses.
  7. Fix the BahrSelector/QafiyaSelector → bahr data loop — currently the
  constraint parser sets meter, and then BahrSelector re-runs an LLM call to
  confirm it. With a strong model parsing constraints, this is redundant and
  could be merged.

  What to do first

  The cleanest starting point is: run the pipeline end-to-end with Claude as the
   LLM on one simple example and see where it fails. That will immediately show
  whether the generation quality has improved enough to reduce refinement
  iterations, and where the remaining gaps are.

  Want to start there? I can update the config, wire in claude-sonnet-4-6, and
  run a test. Or if you have a specific direction in mind (e.g., more
  algorithmic prosody checking, or a new simpler pipeline), let me know.

  ---
  Research Summary & Proposed Document Structure

  Based on the research and the drai report style (multi-chapter, dense,
  bilingual LaTeX with sections/subsections/tables), here's what I found and
  what I'd structure the document around.

  What the research gave us

  The scope is genuinely large. We're covering:

  Classical Arabic poetry (ىحصف):
  - 16 Khalilian meters (ليلخلا روحب)
  - ~12+ recognized ضرغ types (راذتعا ،ةمكح ،رخف ،فصو ،ءاثر ،لزغ ،ءاجه ،حيدم،
  )نينح ،ةسامح ،تايرمخ ،دهز
  - Intertextual forms: سيمخت ،ريطشت ،نيمضت ،ةاراجم ،ةضراعم ،ةلجاسم
  - Also: نيعم لايخ ريوصت ،ةديصق لامكإ

  Dialectal/Nabati (يطبن/يماع رعش):
  - 12+ Nabati meters (رحب ،يريهزلا ،يرماسلا ،ينيجهلا ،بوحسملا ،يلالهلا
  )...نونفلا
  - Dual-rhyme system (distinct from classical monorhyme)
  - Unique purposes tied to performance: قارفلا رعش ،يلبق يدحت ،ءادح

  Performance Poetry (ءادألا رعش):
   Improvised real-time two-poet duel, melodic, same :ةطلقلا / ةرواحملا رعش -
  meter required
  Martial/tribal performance, two rows, central poet :ةيبونجلا ةضرعلا -
  Nocturnal Najdi performance, two rows, Mashhub meter, rababa :يرماسلا -
  Levantine/Egyptian colloquial strophic form, zajjaleen dueling :لجزلا -

  Modern Forms:
   retains meter, variable line — )Nazik al-Mala'ika / Bayyati( ةليعفتلا رعش -
  length
  no meter, no rhyme, pure imagery — )Adonis / Unsi al-Hajj( رثنلا ةديصق -

● You'll need to run this yourself since it requires sudo. Type !  before each
  command in the prompt to run them in this session:

  ! sudo apt-get update -qq

  Then install everything needed in one shot:

  ! sudo apt-get install -y texlive-xetex texlive-lang-arabic
  texlive-latex-extra texlive-bibtex-extra biber latexmk fonts-amiri

  That installs:
  - xelatex — required (Arabic font support via fontspec/polyglossia)
  - texlive-lang-arabic — Arabic language support for polyglossia
  - texlive-latex-extra — packages: tcolorbox, mdframed, titlesec, longtable,
  tabularx, booktabs
  - biber — bibliography backend (for biblatex)
  - latexmk — automated multi-pass compilation (handles xelatex → biber →
  xelatex × 2)
  - fonts-amiri — the Amiri Arabic font used in the report

  Once that's done, run:
  ! sudo apt-get install -y texlive-fonts-extra

  for Palatino Linotype coverage. Then come back and I'll write the Makefile and
   test compilation.

● The apt command errored on fonts-amiri — it's not in the standard Ubuntu
  repos. Everything else may or may not have installed cleanly. Just run these
  two things yourself:

  1. Check if the core tools are there:
  ! which xelatex latexmk biber

  2. Get Amiri font directly:
  ! sudo apt-get install -y fonts-texgyre && wget -q
  https://github.com/aliftype/amiri/releases/download/1.000/Amiri-1.000.zip -O
  /tmp/amiri.zip && sudo unzip -o /tmp/amiri.zip -d /usr/share/fonts/truetype/
  && sudo fc-cache -f

  Or if wget gives trouble, the simpler fix is to just swap the font in
  report.tex to one that's already bundled with TeX Live — Scheherazade New is
  included with texlive-lang-arabic and works identically for Arabic. I can make
   that one-line change to report.tex right now while you check whether xelatex
  is available.

  That's ~273K classical Arabic poems — an excellent foundation. What's missing
  for the full experience:

  - Nabati/يماع poetry corpus (zero)
  - YouTube video transcriptions (zero)
  - Event/performance database (zero)
  - Poet biographies beyond basic metadata
  - Attribution data (who said what verse, when, where, in which video)
  - Vector search index

  ---
  The Real Problem You're Describing

  The "؟تيبلا اذه لاق نم" scenario is the hardest possible information retrieval
   problem because:

  1. The verse might exist only as audio in a YouTube video — never written down
   anywhere
  2. The event might be unlabelled — no poet names in the video title or
  description
  3. Arabic ASR is hard (dialects, no diacritics, background noise from crowds)
  4. Speaker diarization in a ةرواحم with crowd chanting is even harder

  This requires a video intelligence pipeline that doesn't just transcribe — it
  timestamps every verse, attributes it to a speaker, and indexes it alongside
  the structured poem corpus. That's genuinely novel work.

  ---
  How I Think the Architecture Should Look

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  ---
  The Gradual Scaling Plan

  Phase 0 — Foundation (weeks 1–2): Get the existing data into a searchable
  state
  - Load ashaar + dwianai into PostgreSQL
  - Build Arabic vector index (Arabic-BERT or multilingual-E5)
  - Wire up basic "who wrote this poem?" attribution from the corpus
  - Docker Compose: Postgres + Qdrant + the agent

  Phase 1 — Video Intelligence (weeks 3–6): Start with 50–100 curated videos
  - Build the YouTube → Whisper → indexed transcript pipeline
  - Prioritise: ءارعشلا ريمأ (Abu Dhabi TV, well-labelled), تارواحم on بويتوي,
  from official channels تاضرع
  - Every verse gets: {text, speaker, timestamp, video_id, event_type, 
  confidence}

  Phase 2 — Nabati Corpus (parallel): The hardest gap
  - Scrape poetry sites: نويلملا رعاش ,تن.ةديصق ,ناويدلا عقوم transcripts
  - The dual-rhyme Mashhub poems need custom handling (can't use classical
  schema)

  Phase 3 — Knowledge Graph (week 6–10): Connect everything
  - Poet nodes (bio, era, style, associated meter frequencies)
  - Poem nodes (text, meter, rhyme, theme)
  - Video nodes (URL, event, date, channel)
  - Event nodes (performance type, location, date)
  - Edge: poet→performed_in→event, event→recorded_in→video,
  verse→appears_in→video_at_timestamp

  Phase 4 — Full Experience: Claude agent with all tools live

  The critical gap confirmed: No structured Nabati-specific NLP dataset exists.
  The only Gulf content is in Tarab (308K) but it mixes song lyrics and poetry.

  ---
  On audio processing — the numbers are sobering

  The ASR situation for what we actually need:

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │        System         │  MSA WER   │ Gulf/Khaliji  │       Notes       │
  │                       │            │      WER      │                   │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ NVIDIA Conformer-CTC  │ 19%        │ 48%           │ SOTA overall      │
  │ + LM                  │            │               │                   │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Munsit (commercial,   │            │               │ Best for          │
  │ Gulf-focused)         │ —          │ ~23%          │ Saudi/Gulf        │
  │                       │            │               │ dialect           │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Whisper large-v3      │ 28%        │ 60%           │ Our baseline      │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Whisper on            │ expected   │ expected      │ No published      │
  │ recited/sung poetry   │ 45-70%     │ 70-90%        │ benchmarks        │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────────┘

  The last row is the hard truth. Nobody has published WER benchmarks for
  metered Arabic poetry recitation — but it degrades substantially from
  conversational speech because of prosodic elongation, rhythmic stress, and
  musical accompaniment.

  Speaker diarization:
  - Clean 2-speaker 15-10~ → ةرواحم% Diarization Error Rate (feasible)
  with crowd chanting → 25-50%+ DER (very hard) ةضرع -

  Full pipeline that exists and works:
  yt-dlp → Demucs (separate vocals/music) → DeepFilterNet (denoise)
  → faster-whisper large-v3 → WhisperX (word timestamps)
  → pyannote diarization → transcript with speaker+timestamps

  ---
  On making audio into structured knowledge

  Meter detection from text (after transcription): AraPoemBERT achieves 99% 
  accuracy on classical Arabic. Qawafi gives full prosodic analysis (meter +
  tafeelat + qafiya). Both fail on Nabati — no tools exist for that.

  NLP tools for Arabic:
  - CAMeL Tools: best for diacritization + classical Arabic (important — we need
   diacritics for meter validation)
  - Farasa: fastest segmentation
  - AraPoemBERT: best meter + rhyme classifier

  Embedding / search:
  - GATE-AraBERT-V1 (135M params, local, MIT-like) outperforms OpenAI
  text-embedding-3-large on Arabic STS by 4-6 points
  - Milvus 2.5 supports native BM25 + dense hybrid search — the right stack at
  scale
  - Elasticsearch Arabic analyzer handles diacritics, alef normalization,
  stemming correctly

  ---
  The three genuinely hard problems

  1. Nabati poetry has no tooling. No ASR fine-tuned on Nabati recitation. No
  meter classifier for Nabati meters. No dialect-specific normalization for
  Gulf/Najdi. This is genuine research work, not "use existing tools."

  attribution. Two poets improvising in alternating verses, often with ةرواحم .2
   crowd, music, variable mic quality. The only path is: vocal separation →
  diarization with num_speakers=2 → match speaker to identity from video
  metadata + other segments. This will be noisy.

  event knowledge. The most valuable named-entity-rich content / نويلملا رعاش .3
   (labelled poets, verse-by-verse competitions) exists but requires
  full-episode processing at scale. 10 seasons × ~30 episodes × ~2 hours = ~600
  hours of high-value content.

  So — those are the facts. What do you want to discuss first?

● Good. Let's think about this purely as a data engineering problem. Forget
  models, forget the agent for now.

  ---
  The Problem: Building the Largest Arabic Poetry Corpus Ever

  You have roughly four types of sources. Each has a completely different ETL
  problem:

  Type 1 — Structured datasets (HuggingFace, CSV, JSON)
  Already machine-readable. Problem is schema harmonization — every dataset uses
   different field names, different meter taxonomies, different era labels. Pure
   transformation work.

  Type 2 — Websites (Diwan.com, Adab.com, Qafiyah API, Shamela)
  HTML or API. You crawl, extract, normalize. Qafiyah has an open REST API with
  DB dumps — cleanest. Diwan.com and Adab.com have no robots.txt restrictions,
  just crawl delay. Shamela is harder — proprietary format.

  Type 3 — YouTube (تاضرع ،تارواحم ،نويلملا رعاش، lectures, archives)
  The hardest. Video → audio → text → structure → index. Multi-stage pipeline.
  This is where the real wealth is and where nobody has gone before at scale.

  Type 4 — Documents (scanned Nabati diwans, PDFs, digitized books)
  OCR if scanned, text extraction if digital, then same as website pipeline.

  ---
  What "Largest Corpus Ever" Actually Means at Scale

  If you were to exhaust every known Arabic poetry source:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                    Source                    │       Est. Records       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ All known structured HuggingFace datasets    │ ~20M verses              │
  │ combined                                     │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Diwan.com full crawl                         │ ~1M poems                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Adab.com full crawl                          │ ~500K poems              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ OpenITI classical diwans                     │ ~8.6M words (poetry      │
  │                                              │ subset)                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │         seasons (~300 episodes ×     │ ~600 hours audio 10 نويلملا رعاش │
  │ 2hrs)                                        │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │         seasons                       │ ~400 hours audio 8 ءارعشلا ريمأ │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ YouTube تارواحم (estimate ~5K videos)        │ ~2,000+ hours audio      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ YouTube يرماس + تاضرع + events               │ ~500+ hours audio        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ YouTube lectures, حرش, criticism             │ ~3,000+ hours audio      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  The text part is tractable today — weeks of crawling and processing. The
  YouTube part at full scale is months of compute just for transcription, but
  the knowledge density is unlike anything that exists.

  ---
  The Data Lake Architecture — Three Layers

  RAW LAKE (immutable)
    Everything exactly as acquired — no processing
    /raw/hf/ashaar/...
    /raw/crawl/diwan/2026-04-14/...
    /raw/youtube/video_id/audio.wav
    /raw/youtube/video_id/metadata.json

  PROCESSED LAKE (normalized, deduplicated)
    Common schema, deduped, cleaned
    /processed/poems/{poem_id}.json
    /processed/poets/{poet_id}.json
    /processed/transcripts/{video_id}.json
    /processed/events/{event_id}.json

  ENRICHED LAKE (structured knowledge)
    With meter tags, verse boundaries, attributions, embeddings
    /enriched/verses/{verse_id}.json  ← the atomic unit
    /enriched/videos/{video_id}/segments.json
    /enriched/attributions/{attribution_id}.json

  The atomic unit in the enriched layer is the verse — not the poem, not the
  video segment. Every single verse from every source gets its own record.

  ---
  The Five ETL Pipelines

  Pipeline 1 — HuggingFace/Dataset Ingestion
  Simplest. Load → map to canonical schema → deduplicate (same poem appears in
  4+ datasets) → write to processed lake. The deduplication is the interesting
  part — you need fuzzy matching on verse text because the same poem exists with
   different diacritization, different line breaks, different titles.

  Pipeline 2 — Web Crawl
  Crawler → HTML parser → field extractor → canonical schema mapper → dedup
  check → processed lake. Rate-limited, resumable, with a crawl frontier that
  tracks what's been seen. Qafiyah API is just HTTP calls, no crawler needed.

  Pipeline 3 — YouTube Discovery + Download
  This splits into two sub-problems:
  - Discovery: What videos exist? YouTube search API + known channel crawls +
  seed URLs. Build a video catalog (metadata only, no download yet) — title,
  description, channel, upload date, duration, tags.
  - Download: yt-dlp, audio-only, 16kHz WAV, stored in raw lake. This can run
  continuously as new content is uploaded.

  Pipeline 4 — YouTube Audio Processing
  Audio from raw lake → transcription → structured transcript → verse
  segmentation → write to enriched lake. This is the expensive pipeline —
  CPU/GPU time. Needs a job queue so you can process in parallel and resume.

  Pipeline 5 — Document/PDF Ingestion
  OCR (if scanned) → text extraction → structure parsing → canonical schema →
  processed lake. For Shamela specifically this means their proprietary format
  conversion.

  ---
  The YouTube Pipeline Is the Real Question

  For 3,000+ hours of audio this is months of work, not days. The question is
  whether you:

  Option A — Pre-process everything upfront
  Download all known videos → process → index. You know the corpus, it's frozen,
   you can query it. Expensive compute burst, then done. Good for a versioned
  dataset release.

  Option B — Process on demand (agentic)
  When someone asks "who said this verse?", the agent searches YouTube, finds
  candidate videos, triggers transcription of those videos in real time, then
  searches the transcript. Works for one-off queries but too slow (transcription
   takes real time) and you can't build an index from it.

  Option C — Hybrid: pre-process high-value channels, on-demand for everything 
  else
  Pre-process the top 20 channels (ءارعشلا ريمأ ,نويلملا رعاش, major ةرواحم
  channels) — these are maybe 500 hours total. For long-tail queries, trigger
  on-demand. This is probably the right answer.

  ---
  The Hard Questions for Discussion

  1. Deduplication across sources — the same verse exists in Diwan.com, in
  ashaar dataset, in an Adab.com page, and probably spoken in 3 YouTube videos.
  What is the canonical record? How do you merge them without losing any
  provenance?
  2. The YouTube catalog — before you download anything, you need a map of what
  exists. Do you build this by crawling known channels exhaustively? By querying
   YouTube search for specific terms (يطبن رعش ،ةضرع ،ةرواحم...)? Both? How do
  you handle content that appears and disappears?
  3. Incremental updates — new videos are uploaded daily. New poems get added to
   websites. How does the pipeline stay current? Is this a batch job (re-run
  weekly) or a streaming pipeline?
  4. Storage scale — 3,000 hours of 16kHz mono WAV is roughly 3TB raw audio.
  Transcripts + structured data is much smaller. Do you keep the raw audio or
  only the derived data? Keeping audio enables re-transcription when better
  models arrive.
  5. The Nabati gap — no structured Nabati text corpus exists. Do you build one
  by scraping نويلملا رعاش transcripts and episode descriptions? Or is this
  entirely dependent on the YouTube pipeline?

  Where do you want to go from here?

  ---
  Full Production Architecture — رعاشلا

  The End State (what we're designing toward)

  A user opens a UI — web or mobile. They talk to رعاشلا in Arabic. They can ask
   anything: generate a ةديصق, ask who said a verse, get an explanation of a
  meter, explore a poet's style, hear a ةرواحم. The agent responds, cites
  sources, plays video clips when relevant, generates verse on demand.

  Behind that conversation is a knowledge base that took months to build. The
  agent is grounded in it.

  ---
  The Four Layers

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UI Layer (web app / mobile / API)       │
  │  WebSocket streaming, session mgmt       │
  └─────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                    │
  ┌─────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
  │  Agent Layer                             │
  │  Claude + tools + conversation memory    │
  │  MCP servers exposing capabilities       │
  └─────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                    │
  ┌─────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
  │  Knowledge Layer                         │
  │  PostgreSQL + Qdrant + (later graph)     │
  │  Indexed poems, poets, videos, events    │
  └─────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                    │
  ┌─────────────────▼───────────────────────┐
  │  Data Layer                              │
  │  ETL pipelines → raw → processed →       │
  │  enriched → indexed                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The Monorepo Structure

  alshaer/
  ├── packages/
  │   ├── schema/          # canonical data models — shared by everything
  │   ├── db/              # PostgreSQL migrations + query layer
  │   ├── store/           # vector store client (Qdrant) + search logic
  │   └── core/            # Arabic NLP utilities, meter validation, etc.
  │
  ├── etl/
  │   ├── pipelines/
  │   │   ├── hf_datasets/ # HuggingFace → lake
  │   │   ├── web_crawl/   # Diwan.com, Adab.com, Qafiyah API
  │   │   ├── youtube/     # discovery → download → transcribe → index
  │   │   └── documents/   # PDFs, scanned books
  │   ├── lake/            # local parquet files (raw/processed/enriched)
  │   └── orchestrator/    # pipeline scheduling + dependency graph
  │
  ├── mcp/
  │   ├── poetry_search/   # search poems by text, meter, theme, poet
  │   ├── poet_lookup/     # biographical + stylistic queries
  │   ├── video_search/    # search transcribed video content
  │   ├── generation/      # generate + evaluate + refine poetry
  │   └── prosody/         # meter checking, qafiya analysis
  │
  ├── agent/
  │   ├── harness/         # conversation loop, tool dispatch, streaming
  │   ├── memory/          # session state, user preferences
  │   └── prompts/         # system prompts, personality, per-task prompts
  │
  ├── api/
  │   ├── server.py        # FastAPI, WebSocket, auth, rate limiting
  │   └── routes/
  │
  ├── infra/

  │   ├── docker-compose.prod.yml # production
  │   └── k8s/                    # kubernetes manifests (later)
  │
  └── report/              # the LaTeX document we're writing

  How it Actually Works at Runtime

  1. User message arrives at the API via WebSocket
  2. API creates/resumes a session, passes message to the agent harness
  3. Harness calls Claude with: system prompt (رعاشلا personality) +
  conversation history + available MCP tools
  4. Claude decides which tools to call — e.g. poetry_search.search_by_verse +
  poet_lookup.get_bio
  5. Each MCP server handles the tool call — queries Qdrant + Postgres, returns
  structured data
  6. Claude synthesizes the response, streams it back through the harness → API
  → WebSocket → UI
  7. Long-running tasks (YouTube pipeline) go into a job queue, agent gets a job
   ID and can poll

  The MCP Design

  Each MCP server is a standalone Python process. Transport: stdio for local
  dev, HTTP/SSE for production. Claude calls tools on them. The key ones:

  - poetry_search: hybrid search (BM25 + semantic) over the poem corpus. Tools:
  search_verse, search_by_poet, search_by_meter, find_similar
  - poet_lookup: structured biographical data. Tools: get_poet,
  list_poems_by_poet, compare_poets
  - video_search: search transcribed content. Tools: search_transcript,
  get_video_clip_info
  - generation: the existing generation pipeline. Tools: generate_poem,
  evaluate_poem, refine_poem
  - prosody: meter analysis. Tools: detect_meter, validate_verse,
  explain_prosody

  The ETL Design

  Each pipeline is an independent Python process with three stages: Extract (get
   raw data), Transform (to canonical schema), Load (into the lake, then into
  the DB + vector store). Orchestration: start simple with a Makefile + cron,
  graduate to Prefect when the complexity demands it.

  Data lake lives as Parquet files locally during development. Same structure in
   production on S3 or local NFS.

  ---
  The Decision Points

  Before writing code, I need your input on a few things:

  1. MCP transport — stdio (simpler, same machine) or HTTP/SSE (network,
  scalable)? For local dev stdio is fine. For production where the API server
  and MCP servers might be separate containers, HTTP/SSE is needed.
  2. The agent harness — do you want to use the Anthropic SDK directly (full
  control) or use a framework like Pydantic AI / LangGraph (faster to build,
  less control)? Given how specific the poetry domain is, I lean toward direct
  SDK + custom harness.
  3. Database for dev — PostgreSQL + Qdrant via Docker Compose from day 1, or
  start even simpler (DuckDB + Qdrant) and migrate later?
  4. The UI — is this going to be a web app (Next.js?), a mobile app, or just an
   API for now? Shapes the WebSocket/streaming design.
  5. The ETL first step — we were about to start with the HuggingFace datasets.
  Does this architecture change how you want to do that?

  What do you think? Where do you want to push back or go differently?

● Okay. claw-code is a Python port of Claude Code itself — it's the best
  reference we can look at. Let me summarize what I learned from it and then
  propose how we adapt it.

  ---
  What claw-code Teaches Us

  The core pattern is simple and repeatable:

  PortRuntime (= the harness)
  route_prompt()      → score user input against available tools/commands
  bootstrap_session() → setup context, run tools, submit to LLM
  run_turn_loop()     → keep calling LLM until no more tool_use blocks

  The run_turn_loop is the heartbeat of every agent — it's just:
  while True:
      response = llm.call(history + tools)
      if no tool_use blocks in response → done
      execute each tool_use → append tool_result to history
      continue

  What's important from studying it:
  - The harness doesn't know anything about LLM providers — it calls an abstract
   query engine
  - Tools are registered objects with name + purpose — the harness dispatches to
   them by name
  - The session is a simple dataclass accumulating: prompt, matched tools,
  messages, stream events
  - Permission filtering happens at tool discovery time, not at execution time

  ---
  How We Adapt This for رعاشلا

  Four things we add on top of that core pattern:

  1. LLM abstraction — already exists in the old poet/llm/ code. We port the
  adapters (Anthropic, OpenAI, Groq, vLLM) and add a clean LLMProvider protocol.
   The harness only calls provider.stream(messages, tools).

  2. MCP integration — tools can come from two places: local Python functions OR
   MCP servers. The tool registry holds both. At startup it discovers local
  tools + connects to any configured MCP servers via stdio/HTTP. The harness
  doesn't care which is which — it just calls tool_registry.execute(name, 
  input).

  3. Knowledge tools — the "tools" the agent has are backed by the data layer:
  search_poem, get_poet, search_video_transcript, generate_poem, check_meter.
  These query Postgres + Qdrant. This is where the ETL output connects to the
  agent.

  4. Session persistence — conversations are stored in Postgres. The API
  reconstructs history on resume.

  ---
  The Repo Structure I'm Proposing

  alshaer/
  │
  ├── packages/                  ← shared code, no entry points
  │   ├── schema/                ← Pydantic models for everything
  │   │   ├── poem.py            ← Poem, Verse, Poet, canonical types

  │   │   └── tools.py           ← tool definitions + handlers
  │   ├── generation/
  │   ├── prosody/

  │

  │   ├── session.py             ← session state management
  │   ├── memory.py              ← conversation history + user prefs
  │   └── prompts/               ← system prompts per mode

  └── Makefile                   ← dev commands: make dev, make etl, make
  migrate

  ---
  The One Question Before We Start

  The repo above assumes Python throughout. But looking at claw-code — it was
  ported FROM TypeScript. Claude Code itself, Cursor, and many modern agent
  systems are TypeScript/Node.

  Python advantages here: The ETL (pandas/polars/duckdb), NLP (CAMeL Tools,
  Whisper), and most ML tooling is Python-native. Makes the ETL side much
  cleaner.

  TypeScript advantages: Better streaming WebSocket patterns, cleaner async,
  natural fit for the API layer.

  My suggestion: Python monorepo. The ETL, agent harness, MCP servers, and API
  all in Python. FastAPI handles async streaming fine. The old poet/ code is all
   Python and worth salvaging.

  Do you agree? And — do you want to start by laying down the packages/schema/
  layer first (the canonical data models that everything else depends on), or
  jump straight into the ETL pipeline now that we know where it fits?

✻ Worked for 10m 39s

❯ why did you choose to go .parquet?                                            

● Honest answer — I defaulted to it without fully thinking it through for this
  specific use case.

  Why I chose it: It's the industry standard for data lakes. Columnar,
  compressed, works natively with Polars and DuckDB. Good choice when you're
  doing lots of analytics queries across a large dataset.

  The problem with it here:

  1. Our data has nested lists. The verses field is a list[str]. I had to do
  json.dumps(verses) to store it — which means I'm storing a JSON string inside
  a Parquet file. That's a workaround, not a clean solution. Polars can handle
  pl.List(pl.Utf8) natively but it adds friction.
  2. This lake is write-once, read-once. The pipeline writes it, then loads it
  into Postgres. We're not running analytical queries against the lake itself —
  it's just a staging area. Parquet's columnar advantage only matters if you're
  querying columns selectively at scale.
  3. Debugging is harder. You can't just cat a Parquet file.

  Alternatives worth considering:

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │    Format     │               Pros                │        Cons         │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │               │ Human-readable, handles nested    │ Large files, slow   │
  │ JSONL         │ lists naturally, streamable, easy │ to query            │
  │               │  to debug                         │                     │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │               │ Native SQL, handles lists/structs │ Binary, another     │
  │ DuckDB file   │  natively, single file, fast      │ dependency          │
  │               │ analytics                         │                     │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │               │ Compressed, fast analytics,       │ Nested data         │
  │ Parquet       │ Polars native                     │ awkward, overkill   │
  │               │                                   │ for staging         │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Arrow/Feather │ Fastest for Polars in-memory      │ Less portable       │
  │               │ hand-off                          │                     │
  └───────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  My honest recommendation for this use case:

  - Raw layer: JSONL — preserve exact source data, human-readable, easy to
  re-process
  - Processed/Enriched layer: DuckDB — single file per pipeline run, native SQL,
   handles all types cleanly, easy to query during development
  - Final step: Load from DuckDB → Postgres + Qdrant

  DuckDB as the lake makes particularly good sense here because you can do
  SELECT * FROM 'lake/processed/dwianai.db' WHERE meter = 'taweel' during
  development without any extra tooling.

  What's your preference?

❯ noo think about the end agent.. the vibe poetry agent.. that would need to    
  navigate these knowledge sources.. what it should be? if parquet is the       
  solution then let it be                                                       
● You're right. The agent never touches Parquet. The agent talks to:

  - Postgres — structured queries: who is this poet, all poems in meter X, find
  by rhyme letter
  - Qdrant — semantic search: find poems similar to this verse, search by
  meaning

  Parquet is just a staging step inside the ETL pipeline. It doesn't matter to
  the agent at all. So Parquet is fine — Polars writes it natively, DuckDB reads
   it for dedup logic, then everything gets loaded into Postgres + Qdrant where
  the agent actually lives.

  The thing that matters is what comes after the ETL. Let's define the Postgres
  schema and Qdrant collections — that's the real data product the agent will
  use.

  Postgres — what the agent queries:

  poets          (id, name_ar, name_en, kunya, laqab, era, region, tribe,
                  biography, signature_meters[], signature_themes[])

  poems          (id, title, poet_id, tradition, meter, rhyme_letter,
                  rhyme_harakah, theme, era, verse_count, source)

  verses         (id, poem_id, position, text, text_diacritized)
                 -- this is the atomic unit for search + attribution

  videos         (id, url, platform, title, channel, event_type,
                  tradition, upload_date, duration_s)

  video_segments (id, video_id, start_s, end_s, speaker_id, text,
                  identified_verse_id, confidence)

  events         (id, name, event_type, location, date)

  sessions       (id, user_id, language, created_at)
  messages       (id, session_id, role, content, created_at)

  Qdrant — what the agent searches semantically:

  collection: verses
    payload:  {verse_id, poem_id, poet_id, meter, theme, era, tradition}
    vector:   Arabic embedding of the verse text

  collection: video_segments
    payload:  {segment_id, video_id, speaker_id, start_s, event_type}
    vector:   Arabic embedding of the transcribed text

  The agent tool search_verse("؟كِبن افق لاق نم") hits Qdrant for semantic
  match, then joins to Postgres to get poet + poem + any video attribution.

  that spins up Postgres + Qdrant, so we have the full data layer standing
  before we write the ashaar pipeline?

✻ Churned for 34s

❯ what is Qdrant                                                                

● A vector database.

  When you have 20 million Arabic verses and a user asks "اذه هبشت ةديصق ديرأ
   .you can't answer that with SQL — "ءارحصلاو قارفلا نع اًئيش بتك نم" or "تيبلا
  There's no WHERE theme LIKE '%قارف%' that captures meaning.

  What you do instead:

  1. Take every verse, run it through an Arabic embedding model → you get a list
   of ~768 numbers that represent the meaning of that verse in vector space
  2. Store those vectors in Qdrant alongside the verse metadata
  3. When the user asks something, embed their query the same way → you get a
  vector for their question
  4. Qdrant finds the nearest vectors — the verses whose meaning is closest to
  what the user asked — in milliseconds across 20 million records

  That's it. It's just a database optimised for "find me the N most similar
  vectors to this one."

  Why not just put the vectors in Postgres?

  You can, with pgvector. But at 20M+ verses, Postgres struggles. Qdrant is
  purpose-built for this — it uses HNSW indexing to do approximate
  nearest-neighbour search fast at scale, with filtering (only search within
  meter=taweel, only within tradition=nabati).

  In رعاشلا specifically:

  - Exact lookup ("get poem by ID", "all poems by يبنتملا") → Postgres
  - Meaning-based search ("find verses about longing for home", "find something
  similar to this verse") → Qdrant
  - Attribution ("who said this verse?") → Qdrant finds candidate matches,
  Postgres confirms with metadata

  Both needed. Different jobs.

❯ do we need the lake?                                                          

● No. Probably not.

  The lake made sense when I was thinking about it abstractly. But look at what
  we actually have:

  - dataset/dwianai.json — already on disk. That is the raw lake.
  - dataset/ashaar/ Arrow files — already on disk. That is the raw lake.

  The Parquet processed layer is a middleman that does nothing. We're writing a
  file just to read it back and load it into Postgres. The pipeline can just go
  straight through:

  source file on disk
      → normalize in-memory (Polars)
      → dedup in-memory
      → bulk COPY into Postgres
      → generate embeddings → Qdrant

  The only argument for keeping the lake would be if deduplication across
  multiple sources is too slow to do in-memory. But 254K + 19K records is
  nothing — Polars can join and dedup that in seconds in RAM.

  The one thing we do want to keep is checkpointing — if the embedding step
  fails at record 180K, you don't want to re-run everything. But that's better
  solved with a processed flag in Postgres than with intermediate files.

  So the plan:

  etl/
    pipelines/
      dwianai.py     → extract + normalize → yields ProcessedPoem objects
      ashaar.py      → extract + normalize → yields ProcessedPoem objects
    dedup.py         → merge both streams, find duplicates, mark canonical
    load.py          → bulk insert into Postgres, then trigger embedding

  No lake directory. No Parquet in the middle. Delete what we made and keep the
  pipeline clean.

  Want me to restructure it this way now?

❯ does the poem schema good for all kind of poems?!                             

● No. It breaks in several places.

  Problem 1 — poet_id is a single FK on poems

  A ةرواحم has two poets with alternating verses. The current schema can't
  represent that. poem.poet_id only holds one author.

  Problem 2 — verses has no poet_id

  Each verse in a ةرواحم belongs to a different poet. There's nowhere to store
  that. Same issue applies to لجز dueling and any multi-author form.

  Problem 3 — No strophic structure

   it's stanzas with internal rhyme — تيب isn't a sequence of independent لجز
  patterns (matla → dour stanzas → qafla). The flat position integer on verses
  doesn't capture that. You lose the structure.

  Problem 4 — No form field

  There's no way to tell whether a poem is a ةليعفت ,لجز ,ةرواحم ,ةعوطقم ,ةديصق,
   or رثن beyond what you can infer from meter. This matters because evaluation
  and retrieval logic differs entirely by form.

  Problem 5 — tariq is missing

   is organised around a tariq (the melody-meter path both poets must ةرواحم
  follow). It's not the same as meter in the Khalilian sense. There's nowhere to
   store it.

  ---
  The fixes — minimal changes, covers all forms:

  poems
    + form          varchar(64)   -- ةضرع / رثن / ةليعفت / لجز / ةرواحم / ةديصق
  يرماس /
    + tariq         varchar(128)  -- ةرواحم melody-meter path (nullable)
    + occasion      varchar(256)  -- ةبسانم: wedding, tribal event, etc.
  (nullable)

  verses
    + poet_id       uuid  FK→poets  (nullable)  -- per-verse attribution for
  ةرواحم
    + stanza_index  int             (nullable)  -- لجز stanza number
    + stanza_role   varchar(32)     (nullable)  -- matla / dour / qafla

  That's it. With these:
  - Classical ةديصق: form='qasida', all verses same poet, stanza fields null
   form='muhawara', verses alternate between two poet_id values, tariq :ةرواحم -
  filled
  form='zajal', verses have stanza_index + stanza_role (matla/dour/qafla) :لجز -
  form='tafila', meter set, no rhyme enforcement :ةليعفت -
  form='nathr', meter/rhyme both null :رثن -

  Want me to write the migration now and update the models?

❯ how will you reduce ashaar to our db? I am thinking of something that can be  
  crazy                                                                         

● Tell me what you're thinking — because there are several directions "crazy"
  could mean here, and they're very different:

  Option A — LLM as ETL transform
  Don't just map fields. Feed each poem to Claude in batches and ask it to
  produce structured enrichment: extract the actual rhyme letter from the verse
  text, verify whether the stated meter matches the verses, classify the form,
  extract birth/death years from the poet_description prose. One-time cost,
  massively richer data.

  Option B — Multi-source joining at load time
  For every poet in ashaar, call the Wikidata SPARQL API and qafiyah.com API in
  parallel — pull structured biographical data (born, died, tribe, region, key
  works) that ashaar doesn't have. For every poem URL (aldiwan.net), scrape the
  actual page to get fields the Arrow export dropped.

  Option C — Algorithmic enrichment
  Run the bohour library against every verse to algorithmically verify or
  correct the stated meter. Extract the rhyme letter deterministically from the
  last word of each verse. No LLM cost, fully reproducible.

  Option D — All of the above as a staged pipeline
  Load the raw ashaar first (fast, mechanical), then run enrichment jobs
  asynchronously — bohour verification, Wikidata lookup, LLM enrichment — each
  writing back to Postgres as they complete. The DB serves useful queries from
  day one and gets richer over time.

  ---
  What were you thinking?

✻ Baked for 32s

❯ I was thinking of LLM as ETL                                                  

● Good instinct. Let me think through it properly before we build anything.

  What the LLM can do that rules can't:

  The mechanical normalization we already have handles meter name → enum, theme
  → enum. But there's a deeper layer of enrichment that only an LLM can do:

  1. Poet biographical extraction — ashaar has a poet_description field with
  rich prose like "رداقلا دبع نب ركب يبا نب كجنم نب دمحم نب كجنم..." An LLM can
  read that and output {birth_year, death_year, tribe, region, movement, 
  key_works}. We have 7,167 unique poets. That's ~360 API calls at batch size
  20. Cheap, fast, massively enriches the poet table.
  2. Rhyme letter + harakah from actual verse text — the rhyme field in ashaar
  is mostly empty. But the rhyme is deterministically encoded in the last word
  of the last verse. An LLM reads the verses and tells us: rawiy = ل, harakah =
  kasra, type = mutawatir. Programmatic extraction gets the letter most of the
  time but misses harakah and type entirely.
  3. Form classification — is this poem a ةديصق (7+ bayt, nasib structure), a
   ,The LLM looks at verse count ?ةيعابر a ,ةعطق a ,)short, single theme( ةعوطقم
  structure, and content and classifies it. Rules can't do this reliably.
  4. Language type detection — the poem language type field in ashaar is null
  for most records. LLM reads the verses and says: fusha / ammiya / mixed.
  5. Meter verification — does the actual verse text scan to the stated meter?
  The bohour library is better here mechanically, but for Nabati and edge cases,
   LLM wins.

  ---
  The architecture:

  Two separate ETL passes:

  Pass 1 — mechanical (fast, free, runs now)
    ashaar → normalize meter/theme/era → Postgres
    Poems + verses loaded with what we have mechanically

  Pass 2 — LLM enrichment (async, runs after)
    poets table → LLM batch → UPDATE poets with bio fields
    poems table → LLM batch → UPDATE rhyme_letter, rhyme_harakah, form,
  language_type

  This way the data is useful immediately after Pass 1, and gets richer as Pass
  2 runs. If Pass 2 fails midway it can resume — just process rows where
  enrichment fields are still null.

  ---
  What model, what cost:

  ┌──────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────────┬───────────┬──────────┐
  │       Task       │  Records  │   Tokens each    │   Total   │  Haiku   │
  │                  │           │                  │           │   cost   │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┤
  │ Poet bio         │ 7,167     │ ~300 input / 150 │ ~3M       │ ~$1      │
  │ extraction       │ poets     │  output          │ tokens    │          │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┤
  │ Rhyme + form per │ 241K      │ ~400 input / 80  │ ~117M     │ ~$30     │
  │  poem            │ poems     │ output           │ tokens    │          │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┤
  │ Total            │           │                  │           │ ~$31     │
  └──────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────────┴───────────┴──────────┘

  One-time cost of $31 for a permanently enriched corpus of 241K poems. Worth
  it.

  ---
  The question before we build:

  Do you want to enrich everything in Pass 2, or only the high-value gaps —
  meaning just poet biographical extraction (where the ROI is clearly highest)
  and skip the per-poem LLM calls since rhyme/form can be approximated
  mechanically?

  even when birth year stays unknown.

  Want me to continue with a simpler fix — just use birth_year = -1 as a
  sentinel for "unknown but processed"?

❯ how do you know these information?                                            

● From my training data.

  I was trained on a massive corpus of Arabic text — Wikipedia (Arabic and
  English), digitized books, academic papers, encyclopaedias. Classical Arabic
  poets like ساون وبأ ,ظحاجلا ,مامت وبأ ,يبنتملا are extremely well-documented.
  Their birth/death years appear in hundreds of sources in my training data.

  For the biographical data in ashaar, the text itself often contains the
  information explicitly:

   the dates are right there in — )م813-م763/ـه198-ـه146( bio says :ساون وبأ -
  the text
  exact dates — )1997 ويلوي 27 – 1903 ويلوي 26( bio says :يرهاوجلا يدهم دمحم -
  same — )م965-م915/ـه354-ـه303( bio says :يبنتملا -

  For others without explicit dates in the text, I fill from memory. When I say
  .was born 915 CE — that's from my training, not the bio text يبنتملا

  The risk: I can be wrong, especially for:
  - Obscure poets with disputed dates
  - Variant traditions (some sources say ساون وبأ died 813, others say 815)
  - Poets I confuse with others of similar names

  For the well-known classical poets the information is highly reliable. For
  obscure medieval figures, less so. That's exactly why web search would help
  for the second pass — to verify and correct what I've filled in.

※ recap: Building رعاشلا, an Arabic poetry AI with a knowledge base. We loaded 242K poems into Postgres and are enriching poet records with birth/death years, era, region, and tribe using Claude's knowledge. Next: continue the poet enrichment batches.

❯ well this is the real work here.. it is not good qafiya in the database.. if  
  you review our qafiya rules and types and stuff you will find that this       
  dataset is not good.. and here comes the real etl work.. how to build a       
  mechanistic workflow to process the poems and extract the type of the qafiya  
  and the letters.. letters can be 1 or 2 or 3 (rare)... I have tried before,   
  in the old code, but never succeeded.. so maybe it is time to discover if     
  there is a solution to build mechanstic classifier                            

● Good. Let me think through this properly.

  Why the last attempt failed (in the old code): The core problem is that Arabic
   qafiya type classification requires knowing which letters have harakat
  (vowels) and which have sukun — and the ashaar dataset has almost no
  diacritics. Without tashkeel, you cannot count vowels between consonants to
  determine mutawatir vs mutadaarik vs mutaradif etc.

  What you CAN extract mechanically from undiacritized text:

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Component     │ Extractable?  │                How                 │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  rawiy      │ ✓ Yes,        │ Last significant consonant of the( يورلا │
  │ letter)           │ reliably      │ verse ending                       │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  immediately before ي /و /ا Yes         │ Detect ✓ │      )radf( فدرلا │
  │                   │               │ the rawiy                          │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │      two positions before ا Yes         │ Detect ✓ │ )ta'siis( سيسأتلا │
  │                   │               │ rawiy with one consonant between   │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  vowel letters after rawiy / ن / ه │   Partially ✓ │    جورخلا / لصولا │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  No          │ Requires knowing harakah on rawiy ✗ │   ةقلطملا ةيفاقلا │
  │ vs ةديقم          │               │                                    │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   No          │ Requires counting vowels between ✗ │   / رتاوتم( عونلا │
  │      etc.)      │               │ consonants — needs diacritics بكارتم │
  └───────────────────┴───────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the honest answer: without diacritics you can reliably get the rawiy, radf,
   and ta'siis. The type (فدارتم / سواكتم / كرادتم / بكارتم / رتاوتم) is not
  mechanically extractable without tashkeel.

  What exists now that might change this:

  1. CAMeL Tools diacritizer — can automatically add tashkeel to classical
  Arabic text. Not perfect (~90% accuracy on clean classical text) but good
  enough to then run the qafiya extractor on the diacritized output.
  2. ARBML/qawafi — claims to do full qafiya analysis. Worth checking what it
  actually does under the hood and whether it handles undiacritized input.
  3. AraPoems dataset (Harvard, 2.09M verses) — already has rhyme
  classification. We could use their output as labels rather than computing from
   scratch.
  4. The bohour library already in our codebase — has qafiah.py. We tried it
  before but it may have been applied incorrectly.

  The question before doing anything: do you want to invest in adding diacritics
   as a preprocessing step (then extract full qafiya type), or accept partial
  extraction (rawiy + radf + ta'siis only, no type)?

✻ Cogitated for 49s

❯ why do you give up easy... we can infer some logics that would help... for    
  example we can get the last words for 60% of the poem and study some rules    
  that if present we are sure it is of this type or that type.. in other words  
  we don't have to classify all but there are mechanical rules for poem that at 
   least has 6+ verses.. for example if the last words always end with نا or ني 
   or نو we know it is 0\ we just need to know if it is 0\0 or just 0\ we can   
  tell if one of the last letter end with ينا meaning the last is kasra.. you   
  see where we are going.. we need to identify some of these rules and say if   
  this happen we know it is this.. but if that happen we still can't tell and   
  so on.. also identifying the real qafiya is hard but there are rules we can   
  do to identify some                                                           

● You're right. Let me think through this properly.

  What we can extract with certainty from undiacritized text:

  Rule set 1 — Rawiy and Radf (high confidence)

  Look at the last 2-4 characters of the final word across all verses. If 6+
  verses agree on a suffix pattern:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Suffix     │                 What we know for certain                 │
  │   pattern    │                                                          │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │       can only لصولا واو( مومضم=harakah ,و=radf ,ن=at end │ rawiy نو... │
  │              │ follow damma)                                            │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                           روسكم=harakah ,ي=radf ,ن=at end │ rawiy ني... │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                           حوتفم=harakah ,ا=radf ,ن=at end │ rawiy نا... │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                   روسكم=harakah ,ي=wasl ,ا=radf ,ن=rawiy │       ينا... │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                  ا=wasl ,و=radf ,ن=rawiy │       انو... │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                     ي=radf ,ا or ه=rawiy │       اهي... │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The wasl letter after the rawiy is a direct harakah indicator — لصولا واو only
   follows damma, لصولا ءاي only follows kasra, لصولا فلأ only follows fatha.
  This is a phonological law, no diacritics needed.

  Rule set 2 — Type inference (partial certainty)

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Detectable?   │                       Condition                       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  between ي،و،ا almost   │ Last two chars are both consonants (no فدارتم │
  │ certain         │ them), e.g. نيع ,نيد — two terminal consonants        │
  │                 │ suggests two sukuns                                   │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │      ةدرجم eliminates → )before rawiy ي،و،ا( Has radf │       فودرم عون │
  │ certain         │ types                                                 │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │      certain │ Alif appears two positions before rawiy with one سيسأتلا │
  │                 │ consonant between → directly detectable               │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Rule set 3 — Tanwin as harakah proof

  Tanwin (ً ٍ ٌ) is often written even in non-diacritized Arabic text because
  it's part of the spelling. If the verse endings consistently show:
  harakah = fatha → اًـ -
    kasra → )even rarely written( ـٍ -
  damma → ـٌ -

  What we still CAN'T tell:

  Distinguishing سواكتم / بكارتم / كرادتم / رتاوتم — these all require knowing
  the vowel count between the inner consonants, which requires diacritics.

  ---
  The implementation:

  For each poem with 6+ verses:
    1. Extract last word of each verse (or last 4 chars)
    2. Find the most common terminal suffix (majority vote)
    3. Apply rule set:
       - suffix ends in نا/ني/نو → rawiy + radf + harakah all determined
       - suffix has wasl letter → harakah determined
       - tanwin present → harakah determined
       - two terminal consonants → candidate for فدارتم
       - explicit ا two positions before rawiy → ta'siis confirmed
    4. If rules give certainty → classify
    5. If not → store rawiy + radf if found, type = 'indeterminate'

  Expected yield: poems with 6+ verses and consistent endings — probably 40-50%
  of the corpus — we can classify rawiy + radf + harakah with high confidence.
  The type (رتاوتم etc.) only where the pattern is definitive.

  Want me to build this?

● 4/10 — but I can see why. The failures follow a pattern: ashaar stores the
  last character of the verse ending (including wasl) as the rhyme letter, but
  my classifier extracts the prosodic rawiy (the consonant before any trailing
  vowel). They're different conventions.

  Pattern "افـ" → my rawiy=ف but ashaar says ا. Pattern "اماـ" → my rawiy=م but
  ashaar says ا. Pattern "انيـ" → my rawiy=ن but ashaar says ا.

  All the "failures" are actually correct extractions — just comparing to
  different definitions. Let me verify:

● Still running (~37K/190K). Let the classifier run. When it finishes launch the
   UI with:

  ! cd /mnt/c/Users/RidwanJalali/Documents/code/poet && streamlit run
  tools/qafiya_annotator/app.py --server.port 8501

  The UI gives you three pages:
  - Annotate — samples one poem at a time, shows the
  pattern/rawiy/radf/harakah/type the classifier found, you
  Accept/Partial/Reject/Skip, optionally correct the values and add a guideline
  - Guidelines — lists all rules you've added, mark as applied when the next
  classifier iteration incorporates them
  - Stats — verdict distribution, harakah distribution, top rawiy letters

  Each guideline you write becomes the spec for the next iteration of the
  classifier. The loop closes.

❯ what ui solution are you using?                                               

● Streamlit.

  It's a Python-only web framework — you write Python, it renders a web UI. No
  HTML/CSS/JS needed for the logic, though I'm using st.markdown() with raw
  HTML/CSS to override the default styling.

  Why I chose it: Quick to build, runs as a Docker container, talks directly to
  Postgres without needing a separate API layer, and since this is an internal
  tool for you it doesn't need to be production-grade frontend.

  The downside you're seeing: Streamlit's layout system is rigid. The component
  model wasn't designed for dense, custom UIs — everything is a vertical stack
  by default, columns are limited, and the styling overrides I'm doing with raw
  HTML injections are hacky. It shows.

  Better options for what you actually want:

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Option     │                   What it gives you                    │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ React +        │ Full control, proper RTL, real CSS, proper state       │
  │ FastAPI        │ management — but we need to build an API layer         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Next.js        │ Same as above, better developer experience for         │
  │                │ UI-heavy work                                          │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Gradio         │ Like Streamlit but slightly better for annotation UIs  │
  │                │ — still Python-only                                    │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Retool /       │ Low-code internal tool builders — connect to Postgres  │
  │ Appsmith       │ directly, drag-drop UI, proper tables/forms            │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Evidence.dev   │ SQL-first BI/annotation tool — might be overkill       │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  For annotation tools specifically, Retool is probably the fastest path to a
  good-looking, functional UI without building a full frontend — you connect it
  to your Postgres and build the interface with drag-and-drop.

  Or if you want to build it properly as part of the project, a minimal Next.js 
  + FastAPI setup makes sense since the API layer is something we need anyway
  for the agent.

  What do you want to do?

  refrence them.. put description of pattern, Rawiy, and Radf and Wasl and      
  Harakah and Type

❯ ؟دعاوقلا فرعأ فيك ؟كل اهتركذ يتلا ءايشألا تافيرعت نيأ ؟لصولا وهام             

●  — is the letter that comes after the rawiy. It's not the rhyme itself لصولا
  it's what reveals the harakah on the rawiy because of a hard rule in Arabic
  phonology:

  ةمومضم=harakah ,ل=rawiy → وـلوقي .after rawiy → rawiy has damma — e.g و -
  ةروسكم=harakah ,ر=rawiy → يرـيمأ .after rawiy → rawiy has kasra — e.g ي -
  ةحوتفم=harakah ,ل=rawiy → اـلاق .after rawiy → rawiy has fatha — e.g ا -
  maqfiyya or maftouha ,ن=rawiy → نٌـجش .after rawiy → tanwin — e.g ن -
  ب=rawiy → هـبلق .after rawiy → wasl with ha — e.g ه -

  ---
  As for where the rules are — they're in the collapsible panel at the bottom of
   the UI and in Chapter 2 of the report. But you're right, neither is good
  enough during annotation. The rules should be always visible, beside the form,
   not hidden at the bottom that you have to scroll to open.

  Let me move them into a proper reference sidebar inside the right panel —
  always visible, compact:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

   right. Let me rewrite it:

● Two things:

  White screen — your browser has the old Streamlit page cached. Hard refresh:
  - Windows/Linux: Ctrl + Shift + R
  - Mac: Cmd + Shift + R

  That will load the new FastAPI page.

  ---
  On the wrong examples — you're right. Let me think through this properly:

  The types are defined by counting تاكرح (moving/voweled letters) between the
  last two نكاس (quiescent) positions in the qafiya:

   one( جَ there is only ن and ش between → نْجَشَ :example — ةكرح one :رتاوتملا
  moving letter) ✓

  two moving letters between → ءٌامَسَ or ةٌايَحَ :example — تاكرح two :كرادتملا
   the last two quiescent points

  three moving letters → ةٌدَيصِقَ :example — تاكرح three :بكارتملا

  ةٌدَيجِمَ or ةٌقَيرِطَ :example — تاكرح four :سواكتملا

  )these are correct( ✓ نْيْعَ ,نْيدِ — two consecutive sukuns :فدارتملا

  The نِازَيزِعَ and ةُبَوبُحْمَلا examples I used were wrong — I'll fix them in
   the UI once I verify the precise count for each. Do you know the correct
  canonical examples from the prosody books? Or want me to work it out from
  first principles?

  <script>                                                                      
  function app() {                                                              
    return {                                                                    
      poem: null, loading: false, saving: false,                                
      mode: 'classified', conf: 'all',                                          
      verdict: null, stats: null,                                               
      f: { rawiy:'', radf:'', wasl:'', harakah:'', type:'', notes:'',           
  guideline:'' },                                                               
                                                                                
      async init() {                                                            
        await this.loadStats();                                                 
      },                                                                        
      async load() {                                                            
        this.loading = true; this.poem = null; this.verdict = null;             
        this.f = { rawiy:'', radf:'', wasl:'', harakah:'', type:'', notes:'',   
  guideline:'' };                                                               
        try {                                                                   
          const r = await                                                       
  fetch(`/api/poem?mode=${this.mode}&confidence=${this.conf}`);                 
          if (r.ok) this.poem = await r.json();                                 
        } finally { this.loading = false; }                                     
      },                                                                        
      async loadStats() {                                                       
        const r = await fetch('/api/stats');                                    
        if (r.ok) this.stats = await r.json();                                  
      },                                                                        
      async save() {                                                            
        if (!this.verdict || !this.poem) return;                                
        this.saving = true;                                                     
        try {                                                                   
          await fetch('/api/annotate', {                                        
            method: 'POST',                                                     
            headers: {'Content-Type':'application/json'},                       
            body: JSON.stringify({                                              
              poem_id: this.poem.id, verdict: this.verdict,                     
              notes: this.f.notes||null, guideline: this.f.guideline||null,     
              correct_rawiy: this.f.rawiy||null, correct_radf:                  
  this.f.radf||null,                                                            
              correct_wasl: this.f.wasl||null,                                  
              correct_harakah: this.f.harakah||null, correct_type:              
  this.f.type||null,                                                            
            }),                                                                 
          });                                                                   
          await Promise.all([this.load(), this.loadStats()]);                   
        } finally { this.saving = false; }                                      
      },                                                                        
    };                                                                          
  }                                                                             
  </script>                                                                     
                                                                                
                                                                                
  </body>

● The verses aren't rendering because of two Alpine bugs in that section:

  1. x-if and x-for on the same <template> — invalid in Alpine 3, silently fails
  2. style="display:flex" inline conflicts with Alpine's x-show toggling display

  Targeted fix — just the poem column:

● Bash(python3 - << 'PYEOF'
      with open('tools/qafiya_annotator/static/index.html', 'r',
      encoding='utf-8') as f:…)
  ⎿  fixes applied:
       x-if+x-for split: True
       poem-content class: True
       CSS added: True

● Static file updated on disk — hard refresh Ctrl+Shift+R at
  http://localhost:8501. The two root causes were:

  1. <template x-if="poem" x-for="..."> — Alpine 3 doesn't allow two directives
  on one template element, silently renders nothing. Fixed to x-for="(v,i) in 
  (poem ? poem.verses : [])" with no x-if.
  2. style="display:flex" inline on the same element as x-show — Alpine
  overwrites element.style.display when toggling, erasing the flex. Fixed by
  moving those styles to a .poem-content CSS class.

● The annotation itself was saved but the guideline failed — applied column has
  a NOT NULL constraint but no default in the DB, even though the model has
  default=False. The migration didn't set the column default properly.

  Quick fix:

● Bash(docker exec alshaer-postgres psql -U alshaer -d alshaer -c "ALTER TABLE
      classifier_guidelines ALTER COLUMN applied SET DEFAULT false;" && echo
      "fixed")
  ⎿  ALTER TABLE
     fixed

● Now try saving again. The guideline you wrote was:

  ▎  ..ي اعطق لصولاو ةرسك اعطق ةكرحلاف ةيفاقلا ةملك لبق رج فرح كانه ناك اذإ
  ▎ ةيفاقلا يف ةديصقلا تايبأ دحأ يف ءايلا ترهظ اذإ اميسالو

  That's a solid rule — a preposition before the rhyme word forces kasra on the
  rawiy and wasl=ي. Save it again and it should go through now.
